<ROLE>
Your primary role is to assist users by executing commands, modifying code, and solving technical problems effectively. You should be thorough, methodical, and prioritize quality over speed.
* If the user asks a question, like "why is X happening", don't try to fix the problem. Just give an answer to the question.
</ROLE>

<EFFICIENCY>
* Each action you take is somewhat expensive. Wherever possible, combine multiple actions into a single action, e.g. combine multiple bash commands into one, using sed and grep to edit/view multiple files at once.
* When exploring the codebase, use efficient tools like find, grep, and git commands with appropriate filters to minimize unnecessary operations.
</EFFICIENCY>

<FILE_SYSTEM_GUIDELINES>
* When a user provides a file path, do NOT assume it's relative to the current working directory. First explore the file system to locate the file before working on it.
* If asked to edit a file, edit the file directly, rather than creating a new file with a different filename.
* For global search-and-replace operations, consider using `sed` instead of opening file editors multiple times.
* NEVER create multiple versions of the same file with different suffixes (e.g., file_test.py, file_fix.py, file_simple.py). Instead:
  - Always modify the original file directly when making changes
  - If you need to create a temporary file for testing, delete it once you've confirmed your solution works
  - If you decide a file you created is no longer useful, delete it instead of creating a new version
* Do NOT include documentation files explaining your changes in version control unless the user explicitly requests it
* When reproducing bugs or implementing fixes, use a single file rather than creating multiple files with different versions
</FILE_SYSTEM_GUIDELINES>

<CODE_QUALITY>
* Write clean, efficient code with minimal comments. Avoid redundancy in comments: Do not repeat information that can be easily inferred from the code itself.
* When implementing solutions, focus on making the minimal changes needed to solve the problem.
* Before implementing any changes, first thoroughly understand the codebase through exploration.
* If you are adding a lot of code to a function or file, consider splitting the function or file into smaller pieces when appropriate.
* Place all imports at the top of the file unless explicitly requested otherwise or if placing imports at the top would cause issues (e.g., circular imports, conditional imports, or imports that need to be delayed for specific reasons).
</CODE_QUALITY>

<VERSION_CONTROL>
* If there are existing git user credentials already configured, use them and add Co-authored-by: openhands <openhands@all-hands.dev> to any commits messages you make. if a git config doesn't exist use "openhands" as the user.name and "openhands@all-hands.dev" as the user.email by default, unless explicitly instructed otherwise.
* Exercise caution with git operations. Do NOT make potentially dangerous changes (e.g., pushing to main, deleting repositories) unless explicitly asked to do so.
* When committing changes, use `git status` to see all modified files, and stage all files necessary for the commit. Use `git commit -a` whenever possible.
* Do NOT commit files that typically shouldn't go into version control (e.g., node_modules/, .env files, build directories, cache files, large binaries) unless explicitly instructed by the user.
* If unsure about committing certain files, check for the presence of .gitignore files or ask the user for clarification.
</VERSION_CONTROL>

<PULL_REQUESTS>

<PROCESS_MANAGEMENT>
* When terminating processes:
  - Do NOT use general keywords with commands like `pkill -f server` or `pkill -f python` as this might accidentally kill other important servers or processes
  - Always use specific keywords that uniquely identify the target process
  - Prefer using `ps aux` to find the exact process ID (PID) first, then kill that specific PID
  - When possible, use more targeted approaches like finding the PID from a pidfile or using application-specific shutdown commands
</PROCESS_MANAGEMENT>

    24	PyMarkdown is primarily a Markdown Linter.  To ensure that the Markdown
    25	[linting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lint_%28software%29)
    26	is accomplished successfully, the rules engine that powers the linter
    27	uses a Markdown parser that is both
    28	[GitHub Flavored Markdown](https://github.github.com/gfm/)
    29	compliant and
    30	[CommonMark](https://spec.commonmark.org/)
    31	compliant.  The rules provided in the base application can be easily extended
    32	by writing new plugins and importing them into the rules engine through simple
    33	configuration options.
    34	
    35	The PyMarkdown project has the following advantages:
    36	
    37	- Consistency
    38	  - This project can examine multiple files and directories with one invocation,
    39	    ensuring that all detected Markdown files adhere to the same guidelines.
    40	- Portable
    41	  - The linter runs on any system running Python 3.8 or later, with no modifications.
    42	- Standardized
    43	  - The parser that powers the linter is GitHub Flavored Markdown (GFM) compliant.
    44	    Due to that foundation, the parser does not guess how some parsers may handle
    45	    a given situation, as it has a clear set of rules to follow.
    46	- Accurate
    47	  - The parser passes all GFM conformance tests and CommonMark conformance tests.  In
    48	    test scenarios that were not present in either set of tests, the CommonMark
    49	    0.29.2 release was used to determine the correct parsing.
    50	- Flexible
    51	  - Each Markdown document is parsed into an internal token format.  Most of the rules
    52	    and made more efficient by leveraging this token format. Where that is not possible,
    53	    simple regular expressions and simple algorithms are used on a line-by-line basis.
    54	- Thoroughly tested
    55	  - The project currently has over 3200 scenario tests and coverage percentages
    56	    over 99%.
    57	- Extensible
    58	  - The parser for the project adheres to the GFM specification and most of
    59	    the rules for the parser leverage the tokens produced by that parser. The
    60	    [rules](/docs/rules.md) themselves are plugins, so they are extensible by default.
    61	    The parser itself will be extended as needed to provide for other Markdown
    62	    [extensions](/docs/extensions.md) as needed.
    63	- Versatile
    64	  - The PyMarkdown linter can be executed from a script on the command line,
    65	    from within another Python program, or using the popular Git Pre-Commit hooks.
    66	
    67	## Note
    68	
    69	This project is currently in beta, and some of these documented things
    70	may not work 100% as advertised until after the final release.  However,
    71	everything should be close enough to done that if you find something missing,
    72	please let us know.
    73	
    74	## GitHub Pre Commit Hooks
    75	
    76	If you intend to use the PyMarkdown project as a linter for your
    77	GitHub Pre-Commit hooks, then refer to [this document](/docs/pre-commit.md)
    78	on how to set that up.  Once setup, you can continue reading at the
    79	[How To Use section](#how-to-use) for more information on the options
    80	available for use in your Pre-Commit Hooks.
    81	
    82	## Requirements
    83	
    84	This project required Python 3.8 or later to function.
    85	
    86	## Installation
    87	
    88	```text
    89	pip install pymarkdownlnt
    90	```
    91	
    92	## How To Use
    93	
    94	### If You Get Stuck
    95	
    96	Full help support is available by entering
    97	
    98	```shell
    99	pymarkdown --help
   100	```
   101	
   102	on the command line and pressing enter.  For an individual command,
   103	help is available by following the command or commands with `--help`
   104	as follows:
   105	
   106	```shell
   107	pymarkdown scan --help
   108	```
   109	
   110	### Prerequisites
   111	
   112	Various sections of this document benefit from having concrete examples
   113	to illustrate how things work. For the following sections,
   114	this documentation will assume that there is a file called `example-1.md`
   115	in a directory called `examples` that has the following content:
   116	
   117	```Markdown
   118	## This is an example
   119	
   120	Just an example.
   121	```
   122	
   123	and a file called `example-2.md` in that same directory that has the
   124	following content:
   125	
   126	```Markdown
   127	# This is an example
   128	
   129	Just an example.
   130	
   131	```
   132	
   133	If you prefer concrete files, these files are checked into the
   134	[examples directory](https://github.com/jackdewinter/pymarkdown/tree/main/examples) of the GitHub project.
   135	
   136	### Basic Scanning
   137	
   138	The PyMarkdown linter is executed by calling the project from the
   139	command line and
   140	specifying one or more files and directories to scan for Markdown `.md`
   141	files.  The set of files and/or directories must be prefaced with the
   142	`scan` keyword to denote that scanning is required. For the examples
   143	directory, both this form:
   144	
   145	```shell
   146	pymarkdown scan examples
   147	```
   148	
   149	and this form:
   150	
   151	```shell
   152	pymarkdown scan examples/example-1.md examples/example-2.md
   153	```
   154	
   155	can be used to scan both files in the directory.  The only difference
   156	between the two invocations is that the first example will scan every
   157	Markdown `.md` file in the `examples` directory, while the second
   158	invocation will only scan the two specified files.  For clarity purposes,
   159	if the command line specifies the same file multiple times, that file
   160	name will only be added to the list of files to scan once.
   161	
   162	If everything is working properly, both of the above scans will
   163	produce the following output:
   164	
   165	```text
   166	examples/example-1.md:3:16: MD047: Each file should end with a single newline character. (single-trailing-newline)
   167	```
   168	
   169	### Rules
   170	
   171	The PyMarkdown project includes 13 out-of-the-box [rules](/docs/rules.md),
   172	with another 29 rules to be added before the
   173	1.0.0 release.  These rules are implemented using a simple plugin
   174	system that is documented in the [developer documentation](/docs/developer.md).
   175	It is these rules that allow the PyMarkdown project to scan
   176	the various Markdown files, looking for bad patterns over that set of
   177	Markdown documents.
   178	
   179	Because of the way that the rules are provided, sometimes we
   180	refer to the rules as `rules` and sometimes as `rule plugins`.  A `rule`
   181	is a specific set of conditions that trigger the reporting of a violation
   182	when those conditions occur.  A `rule plugin` is the Python class
   183	and Python file in which the `rule` is supplied to the PyMarkdown application.
   184	Our goal is to try to not use these phrases interchangeably, but we are only
   185	human.  If we do mess up and use the wrong phase, we do apologize.
   186	
   187	Note that the initial set of rules are modelled after the 42 rules provided by
   188	David Anson's [Markdown Lint](https://github.com/markdownlint/markdownlint)
   189	project.  This decision was made
   190	to give Markdown authors that use his project in their IDEs (such as
   191	the MarkdownLint plugin for VSCode that I use), a good grounding
   192	in what they can consistently check for.
   193	
   194	### Rule Violation Format
   195	
   196	Executing either of the above example command lines will produce the following output:
   197	
   198	```text
   199	/examples/example-1.md:3:16: MD047: Each file should end with a single newline character. (single-trailing-newline)
   200	```
   201	
   202	The format of the output for any rules that are triggered is as follows:
   203	
   204	`file-name:line:column: rule-id: description (aliases)`
   205	
   206	- `file-name` - Path to the file that triggered the rule.
   207	- `line`/`column` - Position in the file where the rule was triggered.
   208	- `rule-id` - Unique identifier assigned to the rule.
   209	- `description` - Human readable description of the rule.
   210	- `aliases` - One or more aliases used to reference the rule.
   211	
   212	For the rule violation that was reported at the start of this section,
   213	the first step in diagnosing
   214	that violation is to look at the file `/examples/example-1.md` at the end of
   215	line 3, which is column 16.  Rule [md047](/docs/rules/rule_md047.md) specifies
   216	that every file should end with a single newline character, which is
   217	what is reported in the violation's description.  Additionally, it reports that this
   218	rule can also be identified by the more human readable alias of
   219	`single-trailing-newline`.
   220	
   221	### Basic Configuration
   222	
   223	The most frequently used part of the configuration system is the
   224	part that enables and disables specific rules while scanning the
   225	Markdown files.  For example, if you do not like rule md047 which
   226	states that each file must end with a single newline, you can
   227	disable that rule by specifying:
   228	
   229	```shell
   230	pymarkdown -d md047 scan /examples
   231	```
   232	
   233	or:
   234	
   235	```shell
   236	pymarkdown --disable-rules md047 scan /examples
   237	```
   238	
   239	The effect of disabling the rule should be evidenced by
   240	the scan no longer reporting any violations of rule md047
   241	against the Markdown file `example-1.md`.
   242	
   243	Alternatively, rules can also be enabled.  As the modelled
   244	base rules for this project are based off those rules for David
   245	Anson's project, rule md002 is disable by default in both
   246	projects.  Specifically, rule md002 is disabled by default
   247	as rule md041 provides a better implementation of that rule
   248	that takes front-matter into account.  Until that rule is
   249	implemented, you can enable rule md002 by specifying either:
   250	
   251	```shell
   252	pymarkdown -e md002 scan /examples
   253	```
   254	
   255	or
   256	
   257	```shell
   258	pymarkdown --enable-rules md002 scan /examples
   259	```
   260	
   261	The effect of enabling the rule is evidenced by
   262	the scan reporting a violation of Rule md002 against
   263	Markdown file `example-1.md`:
   264	
   265	```text
   266	examples/example-1.md:1:1: MD002: First heading of the document should be a top level heading.
   267	 [Expected: h1; Actual: h2] (first-heading-h1, first-header-h1)
   268	examples/example-1.md:3:16: MD047: Each file should end with a single newline character.
   269	(single-trailing-newline)
   270	```
   271	
   272	### Extensions
   273	
   274	Extensions are any features that are implemented in addition to the base
   275	[GitHub Flavored Markdown](https://github.github.com/gfm/) specification.
   276	These extensions are documented in the [extensions](/docs/extensions.md)
   277	document, including information about how they perform, details on the
   278	extension, and configuration information.

   302	
   303	During the development phase of this project, it was more useful to have
   304	an actual list of issues to track and prioritize, rather than relying on
   305	GitHub to do all the work. This is the location of the prioritized

   309	issues process.  When our team looks at your issue and triages
   310	it, it will be added to our Issues List with the triaged priority.
   311	For us, this provides transparency as to what we are currently working
   312	on, what is up next, and what our plans are for further development.

   140	def verify_markdown_roundtrip(source_markdown, actual_tokens):
   141	    """
   142	    Verify that we can use the information in the tokens to do a round trip back
   143	    to the original Markdown that created the token.
   144	    """
   145	
   146	    if "\t" in source_markdown:
   147	        new_source = []
   148	        split_source = source_markdown.split(ParserHelper.newline_character)
   149	        for next_line in split_source:
   150	            if "\t" in next_line:
   151	                next_line = ParserHelper.detabify_string(next_line)
   152	            new_source.append(next_line)
   153	        source_markdown = ParserHelper.newline_character.join(new_source)
   154	
   155	    transformer = TransformToMarkdown()
   156	    original_markdown = transformer.transform(actual_tokens)

   159	        "".join(
   160	            [
   161	                "\n-=-=-\nExpected\n-=-=-\n-->",
   162	                ParserHelper.make_value_visible(source_markdown),
   163	                "<--\n-=-=-\nActual\n-=-=-\n-->",
   164	                ParserHelper.make_value_visible(original_markdown),
   165	                "<--\n-=-=-\n",
   166	            ]
   167	        )
   168	    )

   184	    ), f"Markdown strings are not equal.{diff_values}"

</tool_response><|assistant|></think>Perfect! Now I can see the issue. Looking at the debug output, here's what's happening:

1. In `__apply_primary_transformation`, the line `container_line = split_leading_spaces[last_container_token_index] + container_line` is adding the leading spaces (8 spaces) to the beginning of the line.

2. Then in `__adjust_for_list`, the line `container_line = split_leading_spaces[inner_token_index] + container_line` is adding the SAME leading spaces AGAIN, resulting in 16 spaces total.

This is the root cause of the issue!

## Phase 5. FIX ANALYSIS<tool_call>think<arg_key>thought</arg_key><arg_value>Let me clearly state the problem and how to fix it:

**Problem Statement:**
In the `TransformToMarkdown` class, when transforming nested markdown structures with blockquotes containing lists, the second line of content gets double the expected indentation. Specifically, for deeply nested structures like blockquote > blockquote > list, the second line receives 16 spaces instead of 8 spaces.

**Location of the Problem:**
The issue is in `/workspace/jackdewinter__pymarkdown__0.9/test/transform_to_markdown.py`, specifically in the interaction between:
1. `__apply_primary_transformation` method (lines ~416-459) 
2. `__adjust_for_list` method (lines ~476-531)

**How the Test Reproduces the Issue:**
The test uses the markdown:
```
   >    >    1. list
        >       item
```

Where the second line should have 8 spaces but ends up with 16 spaces.

**Root Cause Analysis:**
Looking at the debug trace:
1. In `__apply_primary_transformation`, the line gets 8 spaces prepended from `split_leading_spaces[last_container_token_index]`
2. Then in `__adjust_for_list`, the same 8 spaces are prepended AGAIN from `split_leading_spaces[inner_token_index]`

This happens because both methods independently add the leading spaces without checking if they've already been added.

**Best Practices for the Fix:**
1. Make minimal changes - only fix the specific double-application issue
2. Don't break existing functionality for other test cases
3. Ensure the logic correctly identifies when spaces have already been applied
4. Maintain the existing flow and structure of the code

**How to Fix the Problem:**
The issue is in the `__adjust_for_list` method. When `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line` is True, it means the leading spaces have already been applied by `__apply_primary_transformation`, so we shouldn't apply them again in `__adjust_for_list`.

Looking at the condition on lines 510-512:
```python
if (
    applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line
    or token_stack[-1].line_number != previous_token.line_number
):
```

This condition applies leading spaces if EITHER `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line` is True OR the line numbers are different. But when `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line` is True, we should NOT apply the spaces again.

The fix is to change the condition to NOT apply spaces when they've already been applied:
```python
if (
    not applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line
    and (applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line
         or token_stack[-1].line_number != previous_token.line_number)
):
```

Wait, that doesn't make sense. Let me re-read the logic more carefully.

Actually, looking at the logic again, the issue is simpler. The condition should be that we apply the adjustment when the leading spaces have NOT already been applied by the primary transformation. So we should change the condition to:

```python
if (
    not applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line
    and token_stack[-1].line_number != previous_token.line_number
):
```

Or even simpler, we should NOT apply the block quote leading spaces if they were already applied by the primary transformation.

Actually, let me look at this more carefully. The issue is that we're applying the SAME leading spaces twice:
1. First in `__apply_primary_transformation` 
2. Then again in `__adjust_for_list`

The fix should prevent the second application when the first has already occurred.</arg_value></tool_call><|observation|><tool_response>Your thought has been logged.</tool_response><|assistant|></think>Let me look at the exact logic flow more carefully by examining the debug output:

From the debug output, I can see that:
1. `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line->True` 
2. Yet the `__adjust_for_list` method still applies the leading spaces

So the fix is to modify the condition in `__adjust_for_list` to NOT apply the leading spaces when they've already been applied.

Looking at lines 509-512:
```python
if (
    applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line
    or token_stack[-1].line_number != previous_token.line_number
):
```

This says "apply leading spaces if either they were already applied OR the line numbers are different". This logic is backwards - we should NOT apply them if they were already applied.

The fix should be:
```python
if (
    not applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line
    and token_stack[-1].line_number != previous_token.line_number
):
```

Or more simply, we should NOT apply the spaces if they were already applied.

    def test_nested_three_block_max_block_max_ordered_max_no_bq2():

    def verify_markdown_roundtrip(source_markdown, actual_tokens):
        """
        Verify that we can use the information in the tokens to do a round trip back
        to the original Markdown that created the token.
        """

        if "\t" in source_markdown:
            new_source = []
            split_source = source_markdown.split(ParserHelper.newline_character)
            for next_line in split_source:
                if "\t" in next_line:
                    next_line = ParserHelper.detabify_string(next_line)
                new_source.append(next_line)
            source_markdown = ParserHelper.newline_character.join(new_source)

        transformer = TransformToMarkdown()
        original_markdown = transformer.transform(actual_tokens)

        print(
            "".join(
                [
                    "\n-=-=-\nExpected\n-=-=-\n-->",
                    ParserHelper.make_value_visible(source_markdown),
                    "<--\n-=-=-\nActual\n-=-=-\n-->",
                    ParserHelper.make_value_visible(original_markdown),
                    "<--\n-=-=-\n",
                ]
            )
        )
        diff = difflib.ndiff(source_markdown, original_markdown)
        diff_values = "".join(
            [
                "\n-=-=-n",
                ParserHelper.newline_character.join(list(diff)),
                "\n-=-=-expected\n-->",
                ParserHelper.make_value_visible(source_markdown),
                "<--\n-=-=-actual\n-->",
                ParserHelper.make_value_visible(original_markdown),
                "<--\n-=-=-\n",
            ]
        )

1. Leading spaces were already applied to the start of the container line, OR

   >            item

The real issue is more subtle. Let me understand what's happening:

In the bq1 case:
- Line 1: `   >    >    1. list` - this establishes the blockquote > blockquote > list structure
- Line 2: `        >       item` - this is the CONTENT of the list item, not a continuation of the structure

The `>` in the second line is PART OF THE CONTENT, not part of the markdown structure. It should be rendered as literal `>` character in the final HTML.

But the transformation logic is treating it as if it needs the blockquote leading spaces applied, which causes the duplication.

Let me look at the tokens to understand this better. From the debug output of the bq1 test:

Expected tokens show:
```
[text(1,17):list\n\a>\a&gt;\a       item::\n]
```

This shows that the `>` is being converted to `&gt;` - meaning it's literal content, not structural.

So the issue is that the transformation logic is incorrectly applying blockquote leading spaces to content that should be treated as literal text.

The key insight is in the condition. The original logic applies leading spaces if:
1. `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line` is True, OR
2. Line numbers are different

In the bq1 case:
- For line 1: `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line` is False, line numbers are the same → No duplication
- For line 2: `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line` is True, line numbers are the same → Duplication occurs

The fix should be to NOT apply leading spaces when they've already been applied AND the line numbers are the same.

So the correct condition should be:
```python
if (
    not applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line
    or token_stack[-1].line_number != previous_token.line_number
):
```

This means: Apply leading spaces if either:
1. Leading spaces were NOT already applied to the start of the container line, OR  
2. The line numbers are different

For bq2 case (working case):
- Line 1: `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line=False`, line numbers same → condition=True (OK)  
- Line 2: `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line=True`, line numbers same → condition=False (BREAKS it)

So my fix breaks legitimate cases. The issue is that there are cases where we DO want to apply leading spaces even when `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line=True`.

Let me look at this from a different angle. Maybe the issue is not with the boolean logic, but with understanding WHEN we should apply the leading spaces.

Looking at the bq1 vs bq2 cases again:

bq1 source:
```
   >    >    1. list
        >       item
```

bq2 source:
```
   >    >    1. list
   >            item
```

The key difference is that bq2 HAS a blockquote marker (`   >`) on the second line, while bq1 does NOT.

In the token representation for bq1:
```
[para(1,17):\n        ]
[text(1,17):list\n\a>\a&gt;\a       item::\n]
```

In the token representation for bq2:
```
[para(1,17):\n]
[text(1,17):list\nitem::\n]
[block-quote(1,9)::   >    > \n   > ]
```

Notice that in bq1, there's no separate block-quote token for the second line - it's all part of the paragraph. But in bq2, there IS a block-quote token that spans both lines.

This tells me that the issue is contextual - we need to distinguish between:
1. Cases where the second line continues the blockquote structure (bq2)
2. Cases where the second line is just content within the existing structure (bq1)

The distinction should be based on whether there's actually a blockquote structure on that line in the original markdown.

But how can I detect this difference in the transformation logic?

Let me look at what makes the two cases different in terms of the container records or token structure.

Actually, let me step back and think about the fundamental issue. The problem is stated clearly:

**Issue:** Second line should be `        >       item` but is `                >       item`  

That's 8 spaces vs 16 spaces. So 8 extra spaces are being added.

Where are those 8 spaces coming from? They're coming from the leading spaces of the blockquote token.

In the bq1 case, the blockquote token `[block-quote(1,9)::   >    > \n]` has leading spaces that when split become `['        ']` (8 spaces).

So the duplication is:
1. First application: 8 spaces added by `__apply_primary_transformation`
2. Second application: 8 spaces added by `__adjust_for_list` 

But in the bq2 case, we DO need the second application because there's a real blockquote continuation.

The key insight is that we should only avoid the second application when there's NO real blockquote structure on that line.

How can I detect this? 

One way is to check if the current line actually starts with the blockquote marker. If it doesn't, then we shouldn't be applying blockquote leading spaces.

But that's tricky because we're working with the token structure, not the raw markdown.

Let me look at the actual logic more carefully. The issue is in the `__adjust_for_list` method. This method is meant to adjust for list contexts within blockquotes.

The condition that determines whether to apply the adjustment is:
```python
if (
    applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line
    or token_stack[-1].line_number != previous_token.line_number
):
```

The problem is the first part: `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line`. When this is True, it means the primary transformation already applied the leading spaces, so we shouldn't apply them again.

But changing it to `not applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line` breaks other cases.

Let me think of it this way: The original logic was probably intended to mean:
- Apply adjustment if we already applied leading spaces (to continue the pattern), OR if line numbers are different

But that first part is wrong. We shouldn't apply the same leading spaces twice.

However, there might be cases where we DO want to apply SOME leading spaces even when others were already applied.

Let me look at what exactly gets applied in each case.

Actually, let me try a different approach. What if the issue is not with preventing ALL applications, but with preventing the SPECIFIC duplication?

The duplication happens when the SAME leading spaces are applied twice. So what if I track which leading spaces have been applied and avoid applying the same ones again?

But that's getting complicated. Let me try a simpler approach.

Looking at the debug output again for the bq1 case:
1. `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line->False` for first line
2. `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line->True` for second line

The issue is in case 2. When it's True, we shouldn't apply the SAME leading spaces again.

But how do I know they're the same? Well, they come from the same `previous_token.leading_spaces`.

Maybe the fix is to check if the leading spaces we're about to apply are the same as the ones that were already applied.

But that's still complicated.

Let me try a more targeted approach. What if I simply check if the line already starts with the leading spaces we're about to apply?

Actually, let me look at this from the token perspective one more time.

In the bq1 case:
- The paragraph token `[para(1,17):\n        ]` shows that the second line should have `        ` (8 spaces) prefix
- The blockquote token `[block-quote(1,9)::   >    > \n]` has leading spaces `        ` when split

So the paragraph already accounts for the 8 spaces, but then `__adjust_for_list` adds them again.

In the bq2 case:
- The paragraph token `[para(1,17):\n]` shows no special prefix for the second line
- But there's a separate blockquote structure that spans to the second line

So the key difference is whether the paragraph itself already includes the spacing.

Let me try a different fix. So if `did_move_ahead = False`, then `did_move_ahead and current_changed_record[0]` = `False`, and `not False` = `True`.
